### 6.8 Two Lifecycles: Platform and Agents
* **L1 – Platform.** Each deployment target (AWS, GCP, Azure, private cloud, …) has its own ops repository, which pins an exact `agent-factory` commit and deploys it through that repository's CI, run manually on its `main`. `agent-factory` knows nothing about any particular deployment. A platform deploy never adds, removes, or changes agents. An ops repository holds no systems, routes, grants, model catalog or secrets (E10, E11, M3, K5).
* **L2 – No agents in the platform repo.**
* **L3 – Admission gate.** An agent owner registers an agent by naming its repository; admission applies to the agent's whole deployment configuration (SK3, SK4): its source and its adopted skills, each at an approved version. A configuration change that alters which skill versions the agent runs (adopting, upgrading, rolling back or removing a skill) goes through admission again and redeploys the agent; a change that leaves the build hash unchanged (a policy or owner change, a pending request) rebuilds nothing. The factory pins the exact commit and runs the equivalent of CI against it: validate `cartridge.yaml`, build the image, run the agent's own tests (a repository without tests is refused), and check the design rules (§6.6, S1, declared secrets and egress). A failure refuses registration with the reasons, and the refusal is ledgered.
* **L4 – Gated deploy.** An agent can deploy only after a policy owner (e.g. FinOps) has set its policy and budget (E7). The deploy itself may be done by the agent's owner, an admin, or the factory under a policy that allows it. The factory deploys exactly the admitted configuration (SK4): an image built from the pinned source and pinned skills, tagged by a build hash of the pinned source and skills, never a mutable tag; a change to the adopted skills is a new configuration version and so a new image and a redeploy (SK4).
* **L5 – New versions.** The factory notices new commits on a registered agent's repository, and changes produced by factory training, and alerts an admin. Nothing is admitted by itself: an admin chooses to admit a commit, and admission (L3) runs on that action. A version that passes shows as "new version available". It goes live only after a person deploys it, the admin or the agent's owner, from the factory (its API and console, never a CI workflow), unless a person has set a policy allowing that agent's admitted versions to deploy automatically (not built: the first build of this flow has no automatic deploy). (Amended 2026-10-07 by Dale, chat: L5 had the factory run every new commit through admission itself; it now alerts and an admin admits. See `docs/registrar-roadmap.md`.)
* **L6 – Agent template.** `agent-factory` provides the template agent builders start from, including a passing test and the admission requirements. It is documentation and scaffolding, not part of any deployed image.
* **Test fixtures** (such as the echo and summarizer cartridges) are equipment for the platform's own tests. They are not agents and never appear in a factory's agent list. A platform deploy's definition of done proves the agent lifecycle by registering a test cartridge through L3–L4, making one gatekeeper-egress-metered call, and retiring it.

### 6.9 Model Access
* **M1 – One factory model API.** Agents call models through the gatekeeper-egress using the factory's model API: the OpenAI Chat Completions request format, documented for agent builders. Agents never call a provider directly and never hold provider credentials (E1, E5, S1).
* **M2 – Preferred, not chosen.** A cartridge declares a preferred model. The model an agent actually gets is decided by policy (E7); operations may substitute any offered model without changing the agent. A policy that names no models grants exactly the factory's default model (Claude Haiku 4.5 unless operations configure another), never every model.
* **M3 – Operations decides the offering.** Each factory's offering (provider, the provider's model id, price) is factory data that admins maintain, changed without a redeploy; provider keys are supplied through the Keymaster's platform credentials, and a landing zone supplies only cloud permissions (for example the gatekeeper-egress's access to a cloud model service). A preferred model the factory does not offer is shown as a request. Agents never get a route to a model provider other than the gatekeeper-egress's provider routes (E5). The gatekeeper-egress translates the factory model API into each provider's native format and meters every call the same way, whatever the provider.
* **M4 – Native access is an exception.** An agent that genuinely needs a provider-specific feature may declare that need and use the provider's native format through the gatekeeper-egress, still metered and policy-gated. Such an agent is provider-dependent, and its admission shows that.

### 6.10 Ledger Integrity
* **LG1 – Single writer.** Exactly one control-plane process writes a ledger at any time, enforced twice: the control plane holds an exclusive, renewed lease on the ledger before it starts (a second instance refuses to start and says why; a lease left by a crashed instance expires after a short timeout), and every landing zone deploys the control plane stop-then-start, never side by side. A control-plane deploy therefore has a brief gap (accepted 2026-09-28); running concurrent control planes would require moving the ledger off a shared file.
* **LG2 – Never edited.** A ledger that fails verification is archived unchanged as evidence, never repaired in place. Recording continues in a new segment whose first entry chains to the last verified checkpoint and records the recovery (why, and what was archived).

### 6.11 Keymaster: Credentials That Act for a Person or an App
* **K1 – The Keymaster owns them.** OAuth grants, refresh tokens, and app client secrets are held only by the Keymaster. Agents declare the *connections* they need (system plus scopes) in their cartridge; admission shows them; the agent never holds the credential. How each provider's sign-in and tokens work comes from its approved system definition (E10), never from agent or platform code.
* **K2 – Consent happens in the factory.** A person connects an agent to a provider through the factory. The provider redirects back to the factory; the Keymaster stores the grant scoped to agent × provider × scopes; the ledger records who granted what, and when.
* **K3 – The Keymaster refreshes; the gatekeeper-egress injects.** The agent calls a gatekeeper-egress route; the gatekeeper-egress obtains a current access token from the Keymaster (which refreshes and persists rotated tokens) and injects it.
* **K4 – Broken consent is reported, never worked around.** A revoked or expired grant is marked "needs re-consent". A provider that issues no refresh token (LinkedIn) gives a grant that ends when its access token does; the Keymaster reports when it ends, so the person can reconnect before then, and marks it at expiry; the agent is told so and directs the person to the factory's reconnect link. Agents never ask a person to run scripts or handle credentials.
* **K5 – The Keymaster facilitates every credential an agent needs.**
  1. *Declared at registration.* An agent declares every credential it uses, each with its kind (static secret or OAuth connection), the source system it comes from, and scopes where they apply. This includes the credentials the Gatekeeper uses on the agent's behalf: each trigger's `secretRef` and each per-agent route credential (`{agent}_X`). An agent declares a logical name; the Keymaster creates and names every entry itself (`agents/<agent>/<system>/<name>` for an agent's own credential, `shared/<system>/<name>` for a shared one), so an agent cannot name another agent's credential or a platform key. From the declaration the Keymaster knows where the credential is stored and how it is obtained (E8).
  2. *Outstanding credentials are known.* The Keymaster compares each registered agent's declarations with what is present and valid in the secret manager. A credential that is missing, lacks declared scopes, or needs re-consent is outstanding for that agent.
  3. *One flow, every surface.* The Keymaster API is the flow; the dashboard and the Garrison interface are clients of it and add no logic of their own. In each, the owner picks an agent and sees its outstanding credentials, each with instructions for creating it and a way to supply it: OAuth through a consent URL whose callback lets the Keymaster store the grant; static secrets (for example GitHub PATs, Discord bot tokens, Slack keys, API keys) through a write-only input the Keymaster writes to the secret manager. A supplied value is never displayed, echoed, logged, or ledgered; the ledger records only that it was set or rotated, by whom, and when.
  4. *Instructions come from a reviewed catalog.* The Keymaster keeps a catalog of instructions per source system, maintained by operations. Entries may be AI-drafted; a person reviews and approves each one. Until then an entry is shown to owners clearly labelled "Pending human review", never presented as approved.
  5. *Access is policy, not possession.* A credential may serve several agents (for example one workspace integration). Because agents never hold it (S1), which agents may use it is decided by factory policy (routes and agent state), not by who has the value: revoking one agent's access (pause, decommission, destroy, or removing the route) never requires rotating the credential, and rotating a credential never involves an agent.
  6. *Addressed to the agent owner* (an owner and an admin may supply an agent's credentials; owner rights are the Bouncer's, GAP-088). No one is asked to run a script or to put a secret in a chat, terminal, or repository.


### 6.12 Operator Access
* **A1 – One front door.** The factory's public endpoints accept connections only from its identity-aware proxy (Cloudflare Access on AWS); the load balancer refuses all other sources. Machine callers (deploy checks, webhooks) use the same door with a service credential. No landing zone opens platform ingress to `0.0.0.0/0`.
* **A2 – Identity is verified, not asserted.** The control plane grants a role only from a credential it verifies cryptographically: a factory token, a run token, or the proxy's signed assertion checked against its issuer and audience. An identity header alone grants nothing, and no proxy (the console included) adds a privileged credential on a caller's behalf.


### 6.13 Recoverability
* **R1 – The factory's own state is recoverable.** Everything only the factory holds (agent registrations and deployment configuration, policies, approvals, schedules, runs) is backed up automatically and can be restored to an earlier point in time. The ledger (§6.10), credentials (§6.11) and agent memory (§6.6) keep their own protection. Restoring is a documented operation, rehearsed at least once. The deployment configuration store (SK3) is versioned with point-in-time restore and can be exported.


### 6.14 Skills and Deployment Configuration
* **SK1 – Skills are registered, admitted and approved per version.** Any authenticated user may register a skill by naming a repository, a path and a commit (registering a private skill needs an admin or an owner of the named agent): one repo per skill, a skills monorepo, or a folder inside an agent's repo. A skill is its source: a different repository or path is a different skill, never a new version of an existing one. A skill is **public** (any agent may be given it, SK3) or **private** (it names one owner agent in its registration and only that agent may adopt it). Visibility and owner are set at registration and never change; to change either, register a new skill. Admission pins the commit, runs the skill's tests and checks the design rules (no secrets, no direct hosts, no provider SDKs). Every version, public or private, not just the first, is approved by a factory admin before any agent can adopt it, and only after the factory has built it, run its tests and checked its code against these rules, as a pull request merges only when its required checks pass. Approving a version makes it *available*; it adopts nothing for a public skill (SK3). Refusals, approvals and retirements are ledgered.
* **SK2 – A skill declares what it needs and what it does, and holds nothing.** A skill's manifest declares the routes, connections (with scopes), credentials and models it requires, and its **actions**: each action names the system route, method and path it takes (E11) and says `hold: true` or `hold: false`. `hold: true` means the action is human-in-the-loop: the gatekeeper-egress forwards nothing until a person has approved that exact request, and the approval is written to the ledger before the action is allowed (E9). `hold: false` means autonomous. A manifest with an action that declares no `hold` is refused (no default, E9). Today the gatekeeper-egress cannot tell actions on one route apart (GAP-070), so a declared `hold` is recorded and shown to the approver but is enforced only once grants carry actions (E11, TSK-068). A skill never holds a secret (S1) and never grants access; what it can do inside an agent is bounded by that agent's policy (E7). The skill's declared holds are the author's declaration (E9); an admin reviews them at version approval and may change them, and may change any grant's hold at any time. Enforcement is per agent; a skill that must be isolated from its host's other grants runs as a separate service with its own identity.
* **SK3 – An agent's deployment configuration lives in the factory, and adoption is part of it.** Each agent has one versioned configuration record: its pinned source (repo and commit), the skills it has adopted (each pinned to an approved version), and its policy. Every change creates a new version recording who, when and why, and is ledgered with a content hash. Adopting, upgrading, holding, rolling back or removing a skill is a configuration change made in the factory, never a pull request or other change in `agent-factory`, `submind-aws` or any agent or skill repository. Adopting a **public** skill is requested by the agent's owner (or, from conversation, by the agent, SK4) for a named agent and an approved version; the request always needs an admin's decision (the admin who sets policy, E7) and takes effect only when approved. Approving an adoption does not grant access: the agent's policy still names the routes (SK2). A **private** skill is adopted for its owner agent automatically, in the same configuration change as the approval of each of its versions, unless that agent's adoption was revoked (SK6); no one may adopt it for any other agent, and the factory refuses the attempt. The admin who approves the version is recorded as the approver of the adoption, and the adoption has its own ledger row, marked automatic. Approving a version (SK1) and approving an agent's adoption of it are separate decisions with separate ledger rows; neither implies the other for a public skill. A pending adoption request is not part of the configuration: it creates no configuration version and no build, and only an approved adoption (or a removal) writes a new version and changes the build hash (SK4).
* **SK4 – What runs is exactly the configuration; a change redeploys.** Applying a configuration builds one image from the agent's source at its commit plus each adopted skill at its pinned commit, tagged by the source commit and a hash of the pinned skills (never the configuration hash, so a policy or owner change never rebuilds; an agent with no skills keeps the tag `<agent>-<commit>` it always had), admitted and deployed (L3, L4). The Landlord builds the image, places each skill in it with a manifest of the adopted skills, and names that manifest in the agent's environment at launch; the factory delivers the files and the manifest, and how an agent invokes a skill is the cartridge's (the Donna Test). No code is ever loaded at runtime and nothing needs a pull request: a new configuration version triggers the build and redeploys the agent, which restarts to activate it. An agent's request in conversation to adopt a skill creates an adoption request (SK3) that a person approves. An agent whose configuration did not change is not rebuilt or restarted.
* **SK5 – New skill versions are offered, never forced.** When a newer approved version of an adopted public skill exists, the factory shows "skill update available" to the agent's owner (as L5 does for agents). The owner decides when to upgrade. A private skill's newly approved version is adopted by its owner agent automatically (SK3) unless the owner agent's adoption was revoked (SK6); that is the one exception.
* **SK6 – Skills are retired and adoptions revoked separately.** An admin may **retire** a skill: no new version can be approved or adopted, and every approved version stays recorded. Retiring a skill, or revoking one approved version, that any agent's deployed configuration uses is refused until those agents are redeployed without it; an admin override retires or revokes it at once, pauses every agent using it, removes it from each configuration and redeploys each without it (SK4), and the agent is resumed when that completes. An agent's owner or an admin may **revoke one agent's adoption** of a skill: that writes a new configuration version without it (SK3) and redeploys the agent (SK4). Revoking an adoption of a private skill by its owner agent leaves the skill registered and unadopted, and stops its later versions from being adopted automatically; the owner agent adopts it again only by a new adoption request that an admin approves (SK3), because a revocation is a deliberate decision that a new version must not silently undo. Retirements, revocations and the pauses they cause are ledgered.
* **SK7 – The factory shows what is registered and who uses it.** A factory API returns every registered skill with its visibility (public or private), its owner agent (private only), its versions with status, check results and declared actions and holds, and the agents that have adopted each version (and any adoption requests awaiting approval). The same API answers the reverse: the skills one agent has adopted. The console is a client of that API only, and from it an authorized person registers a skill, approves or rejects a version, retires a skill, requests, approves and revokes an agent's adoption, and sees "update available" (SK5). Every one of those actions asks the Bouncer for a named privilege (§6.15). A private skill is visible only to admins and to the owners of its owner agent.
* **SK8 – Existing in-repo skills are private skills until split.** A skill that an agent carries and declares inline in its own `cartridge.yaml` (today's agents) is treated as a private skill of that agent. It is registered, approved per version and adopted under SK1 to SK4 like any other, and the inline declaration is retired when it is. A skill that more than one agent should use is split out as a public skill by registering it as a new skill (SK1); that is later, separate work. Until an agent's skill is registered, the agent's own repository remains its only source for it (E8) and the registry shows it as unregistered.

### 6.15 Factory Service Architecture (The Cast of Characters) & First-Class Contracts
* **SV1 – Intentional Services.** Every workspace package belongs to one of the 11 recognized services (Gatekeeper, Keymaster, Executive, Secretary, Landlord, Auditor, Treasurer, Bouncer, Timekeeper, Registrar, Inspector), to declared platform tooling (`console`, `conformance`, `bench`), or to a declared reservation for a named service that has no implementation yet (none today). A service's `hostedIn` entries are workspace packages that contain its implementation source; a directory that merely exists is not a host. Introducing a new service requires explicit architectural intent, naming the new cast member, and user confirmation before implementation. Incidental or stealth service creation is prohibited.
* **SV2 – First-Class Cross-Service Contracts.** Services communicate across boundaries strictly through explicit, typed contracts wired directly into real call sites. Direct backdoor access to another service's private persistence, tables, files, or internal modules without a formal contract is forbidden.

#### The 11 Factory Services (The Cast):
1. **Gatekeeper (Perimeter Security):** Owns inbound presence, wakes, webhooks, front-door authentication verification (A1, A2), and zero-trust outbound proxying (E1, E6) with credential injection (S1). Composed of `gatekeeper-ingress` and `gatekeeper-egress`. It authenticates and never authorizes: `packages/auth` verifies a credential and yields a principal with its roles (A2), and the identity links an admin sets (a Discord user to a person) are its data; what a principal may do is the Bouncer's.
2. **Keymaster (Credential Facilitation):** Owns OAuth connections, consent flows, token refreshes (K1–K4), write-only static secret onboarding (K5), and vault mapping.
3. **Executive (Model Service / Inference Provider):** Provides the uniform OpenAI-compatible Chat Completions API (M1), translates calls to model providers (Bedrock, OpenAI, Anthropic, xAI), enforces model policy routing (M2), and counts tokens (E5); it reports each call's usage and the Treasurer prices it and decides standing.
4. **Secretary (Hydration & State Store):** Enforces "The Safe" (§6.6); syncs local SQLite databases in `$MEMORY_DIR` with cloud object storage on wake and sleep, ensuring cartridges never require cloud storage SDKs.
5. **Landlord (Compute Lifecycle & Turn Broker):** Manages serverless min=0 compute, wake-from-zero, warm-down windows, operational pause/kill-switches, turn coordination via `/mailbox` long-polling, and two-stage retirement (§6.4).
6. **Auditor (Immutable Ledger):** Owns the cryptographic append-only WORM audit trail of runs, spend, credential actions, and perimeter decisions (LG1, LG2) with automatic secret and prompt redaction.
7. **Treasurer (Spend Governance & FinOps):** Owns the budget rule in `packages/budget` and the factory cloud spend, compute inventory reporting, and factory budget governance. `checkStanding` is the typed contract for whether an agent is inside its limits (unacknowledged spend included when the caller has it). `packages/budget` holds the budget rule, spend accounting, pricing, the standing contract, cloud cost aggregation, compute inventory attribution, and factory-wide budget tracking. The Treasurer holds dedicated read-only cloud credentials (`factory-prod-treasurer` role) for Cost Explorer and ECS cluster inventory; agents never hold direct AWS routes or credentials. The control plane exposes the Treasurer's reports via `GET /api/v1/spend/report`, `GET /api/v1/spend/compute`, `GET /api/v1/spend/budgets`, and `PUT /api/v1/spend/budgets`. Model spend and cloud infrastructure spend are covered by the single `factory-spend` tool grant. Budgets are evaluated on gross usage (before credits). There is no default budget (Dale, chat, 2026-10-10): neither the factory's monthly budget nor any agent's is created by default or seeded by code; an admin allocates each explicitly through the API (E7), and until one is set nothing is enforced or alerted on for it. Agent compute is reporting-only. The gatekeeper-egress asks before every model call, the Landlord asks before every wake (`createRun`), the control plane asks before a message goes into a live run, and it also asks on ledger ingest and on a policy update. Those callers enforce the answer (`budget.alert`, `blockRun`, HTTP 402); the Gatekeeper's ingress relays a refused wake or message to the person. A conformance test fails if the gatekeeper-egress or control-plane source compares spend with a limit itself.
8. **Bouncer (Authorization & Approvals):** `packages/bouncer` decides who may do what and holds what must wait for a person. Authorization is role-based in every case: a user maps to roles and a role maps to privileges, never a user to a privilege, and the table is built into the code (making it factory data an admin edits is a later enhancement). Every control-plane route asks the Bouncer for one named privilege (`authorize`, `requirePrivilege`); the roles are the six `auth` emits plus two derived from data, never from a credential: `agent-owner` (a person on the agent's configuration record, SK3) and `requester` (the person whose message started a run, set only by the ingress). An owner may wake, pause and resume the agent, view and export its configuration, supply its credentials and start its connections, and decide its held actions when the run has no requesting user. For an agent's action the agent is the actor and the requesting user is the reviewer and approver; the global `admin` and `approver` roles also decide any agent's. The Bouncer also owns the approval record and the E9 hold rules (the reviewed copy, its hash and size limit, which requests are held and which tool calls need approval); the gatekeeper-egress asks it and enforces the answer, and the control plane acts on a decision (`blockRun`, `deliverDecision`). Conformance fails on a role check outside `packages/bouncer` and `packages/auth`, on a privilege that is not in the contract, and on a route the matrix test does not list (`packages/conformance/src/authorization.test.ts`).
9. **Timekeeper (Scheduling & Timers):** Decides when an agent is due, without persistent in-container daemon processes. `packages/timekeeper` holds the cron rule (`cronMatches`, `agentsDueForCron`), time zones and cron validation (`getZonedTimeParts`, `isTimeZone`, `cronIssue`, `DEFAULT_TIMEZONE`), the schedule record and its store (`ScheduledAction`, `ScheduleStore` with `checkDue`, persisted as `schedules.json` at `FACTORY_SCHEDULES_PATH`, else `DATA_DIR/schedules.json`), and the contract and the clock: `createTimekeeper({ agents, schedules, fire })` asks every minute which cartridge crons and which schedules are due and calls `fire` with a `FireRequest` (`{ kind: 'cartridge', agentId }` or `{ kind: 'schedule', schedule }`). It never starts a run and never imports the control plane; the composition root (`control-plane/src/index.ts`) is the only caller, and its `fire` is the Landlord's side (`createRun`, and the rule that a cartridge cron is skipped while the agent is already running). What stays outside: the `/api/v1/schedules` handler stays in `control-plane/src/schedules.ts` and owns the run-token scoping (the Gatekeeper's, GAP-089), the Bouncer privilege calls and the ledger rows (the Auditor's), because those meet a request there. One-shot wakeup timers are not built (GAP-096). A conformance test (`packages/conformance/src/timekeeper.test.ts`) fails on a second copy of the cron rule, a second scheduler loop or a back-dependency.
10. **Registrar (Admissions & Catalog):** Decides what is admitted and what is recorded about it, without building an image or serving a request. `packages/registrar` holds the agent record and the built-in agents (`AgentRecord`, `loadCatalog`, `loadDynamicRegistry`, `mergeAgents`), the registry store (`AgentRegistry`: `save`, `update` and `remove` over `<registryDir>/<id>.json`), source pinning (`FULL_SHA`, `checkRepoUrl`, `gitSkillSource`), the factory's checks on a skill's code (`SkillChecker`, `localSkillChecker`, `SKILL_CHECK_SCRIPT`), the skill registry (`SkillRegistry`, `summarizeSkill`, `compareSemver`, `checkSkillPath`, `fetchManifest`), the deployment configuration store (`VersionedConfigStore` over a directory or S3 backend, `configHash`, `archiveStamp`) the system definition store (E10: `SystemsStore`, written through to `<dir>/<id>/<version>.json`, with a ledger row for each proposal, decision and import) and the admission contract (`pinSource`, `beginAdmission`, `admit`, `admissionOf`, `stateAfterRefusal`). `admit` takes a source and the Landlord's build callback, changes no record and cannot deploy, so a deploy today and an admin admitting a newly seen commit later (L5) call the same function. What stays outside: the HTTP handlers (`handleConfig`, `handleSkills`, `handleSystems` and the registry and deploy routes in `app.ts`), because that is where the Bouncer's privileges, the Auditor's ledger rows and the Gatekeeper's run-token rule meet a request (GAP-099); the image build, identity and compute registration (the Landlord's); `skillCheckerFromEnv`, which picks the deployment's skill checker and loads the Landlord's CodeBuild one (`control-plane/src/skills.ts`); registration, which still pins inline (GAP-101); orphan pruning, which reads the registry and the Gatekeeper's policy store together (GAP-100); and the two methods that turn a system's OAuth description into a Keymaster connection provider, which are on a subclass of the store in `control-plane/src/systems.ts` so that the Registrar does not depend on the Keymaster (D2, Dale's option A). A conformance test (`packages/conformance/src/registrar.test.ts`) fails on a second copy of these rules, an admission record built by hand, a write to the registry directory outside the store, or a back-dependency. The agent version flow (a new commit alerts, an admin admits it, the admin or the owner deploys it; L5, GAP-037) is not part of the extraction: `docs/registrar-roadmap.md`.
11. **Inspector (Observability, Telemetry & Triage):** Watches the factory and says what it sees, without deciding anything or answering a request. `packages/inspector` holds the contract (`createInspector`: `publish`, `publishRun`, `subscribe`, `tapLedger`, `attachBus`, `reportProgress`, `readProgress`, `stop`), the event hub and the bus sinks (`EventHub`, `isBusWorthy`, `fileSink`, `eventBridgeSink`, `busSinkFromEnv`, `attachBus`), the per-run progress ring and its sanitizer (`RunProgress`, `sanitizeProgress`), the egress's progress emitter (`ProgressEmitter`, `ProgressCall`, `safeIdent`), prompt traces (`writeTrace`, `pruneTraces`, `traceConfigFromEnv`), the run metrics instruments (`factoryMetrics`) and the triage rule (`incidentsFromRuns`). `packages/telemetry` (`initTelemetry`) stays a separate package the Inspector hosts (D-A). What stays outside: the HTTP handlers (`control-plane/src/progress-routes.ts`, `stream.ts`, and the `/api/v1/metrics` and `/api/v1/triage` routes in `app.ts`), because that is where the Bouncer's privileges and the Gatekeeper's run-token rule meet a request (GAP-104); the `/api/v1/metrics` aggregate, which reads five members' data (GAP-106); the egress's own request, latency, token and cost instruments (GAP-109). The empty `packages/triage` placeholder (a README for an alert router that was never built) was removed (D-B); the router is GAP-107. The Inspector does no authorization and never reads a run: the caller passes in whether a reported run is live. `tapLedger` still wraps the Auditor's store (GAP-105). The role text used to say it routes crash diagnostics to alerting; what exists is the derived incident list and the bus sink (GAP-107). A conformance test (`packages/conformance/src/inspector.test.ts`) fails on a second copy of these rules, a second Inspector, or a back-dependency.

#### Architectural Boundaries:
* **The Cartridge vs. Factory Boundary ("The Donna Test"):** Cartridges (the employees) own business logic, prompts, persona, and internal cognitive reasoning loops. The Factory provides the utilities (compute, memory safe, model service, perimeter).
* **The Console vs. Factory Boundary (Headless Principle):** The web Console and Garrison are headless client overlays (§6.5). They own zero service logic, zero secrets, and zero persistence.

#### Cast extraction roadmap:
Each member that shares a package is extracted into its own package behind a typed contract, one member at a time (the Treasurer flow, GAP-083, is the worked example). A change is then inside one member or on one contract.

**Order** (recorded 2026-10-03; revise a step when its turn comes and its real dependencies are visible):

| Step | Member | Why here |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 0 | Contract location convention and file-to-member map (TSK-091 decided; TSK-092, TSK-093 enforce) | Every extraction needs both; TSK-087 deferred them. |
| 1 | Treasurer (GAP-083, TSK-080, TSK-084 to TSK-090) | Done; set the pattern. Follow-ups: GAP-091 and TSK-113 (a message into a live run asks the Treasurer first); GAP-110 and TSK-142 (cloud spend, compute inventory and factory budget governance). |
| 2 | Bouncer (authorization) | Every later extraction moves routes out of `app.ts`; if each route already declares its privilege through one contract, moved routes arrive authorized and a route-coverage check protects every later move. Done: GAP-086 to GAP-088, TSK-094 to TSK-109 (the Bouncer flow work stream); GAP-089 records what stays outside. |
| 3 | Timekeeper | `schedules.ts` is already its own file and nothing depends on it; a low-risk second use of the template. Done: GAP-095, TSK-117 to TSK-123 (the Timekeeper flow work stream); moves only. GAP-096 (one-shot timers) and GAP-097 (the handler's import cycle with `app.ts`) record what stays outside. What an agent can and cannot do with schedules, and the order to fix it, is in `docs/timekeeper-roadmap.md`. |
| 4 | Registrar | `catalog.ts`, `skills.ts`, `skill-checks.ts` and `config-store.ts` are cohesive and large; takes about 2,000 lines out of the control plane. Done: GAP-098, TSK-124 to TSK-132 (the Registrar flow work stream; moves only, apart from the guard test; PRs #104, #105, #107, #108, #109 and the systems store's). GAP-099 to GAP-101 record what stays outside. The version flow that belongs to the Registrar but is not part of the extraction (a new commit alerts, an admin admits, the admin or owner deploys from the console; GAP-037, TSK-028) is in `docs/registrar-roadmap.md`. |
| 5 | Inspector | A leaf: nothing depends on observability. Done: GAP-103 to GAP-109, TSK-135 to TSK-141 (the Inspector flow work stream; 7 tasks in 4 PRs; PRs #112, #114, #115 and the close-out's). Moves only, apart from the contract's wiring and the guard test. GAP-104 to GAP-109 record what stays outside. Decisions in "Inspector decisions" below. |
| 6 | Executive | After the Treasurer takes pricing, what remains is provider translation and token counting, which leaves `gatekeeper-egress` as perimeter only. Planned 2026-10-09: GAP-119, TSK-147 to TSK-155 (the Executive flow work stream below); moves first, then `checkModel`, `offeredModels` and `complete` as the contract. Dale's model-selection intent (cartridge and skill hints, owner-chosen primary and secondary, the Executive serves) is recorded in `docs/executive-roadmap.md` and is planned after this extraction, not inside it. |
| 7 | Landlord | Last in the control plane: working assumption is that it is what remains (runs, `createRun`, runtime, wake). Confirm when reached. |
| 8 | Gatekeeper egress | Once Executive, Treasurer and Inspector pieces are out, egress is the perimeter; ingress and `auth` are already separate. |

Auditor (`ledger`), Secretary (`hydrate`) and Keymaster (`keymaster`, `secrets-bind`) already have packages; they need only SV2 contract wiring (GAP-080).

#### Cast extraction decisions (Dale, chat, 2026-10-03; TSK-091):
- **D1, where a member's contract lives: in the member's own package, entry point locked (option C).** The member's root `index.ts` exports the contract (a typed function and its request and result types, as `checkStanding` does in `packages/budget`). Callers depend on that package. Its `package.json` `exports` map allows only the root (plus any subpath listed as part of the contract), so a deep import is a build error. `packages/contract` stays what it is: cartridge schema and validation, and the Cast names. Watch dependency order; two members that call each other need a decision before extraction.
- **D2, file-to-member map: machine-readable (M2).** An `owns` list per member in `packages/contract/src/services/types.ts`, with a conformance test that every non-test source file in `control-plane/src` and `gatekeeper-egress/src` is owned (a file several members share is marked `split` until it is broken up). **The map below is now machine-checked (TSK-093, 2026-10-06) against `FACTORY_SERVICES[member].owns`, `SPLIT_FILES`, `COMPOSITION_ROOT_FILES` and `PLATFORM_FILES` in `packages/contract/src/services/types.ts`; the code is the source of truth and a change to either must change both. Dale was short of time and may still revise it; TSK-093 added `aws/*`, `gcp/*` and `identity-links.ts`, which the first draft did not list, and resolved `ui.ts` and the inferred rows by reading the files (see the 2026-10-06 change log row).**
  - **File-ownership rule (Dale, chat, 2026-10-06; confirm the wording):** a file belongs to the member whose own code is in it. Calling another member's contract (`checkHold`, `checkStanding`) does not make the caller a co-owner; that is what a contract is for. A file is shared (`split`) only while another member's code is still inline in it, and stops being shared when that member is extracted. `gatekeeper-egress.ts` is the Gatekeeper's (the perimeter, §6.15 roadmap step 8) and is shared with the Executive until its extraction (step 6), because the model route is inline in it. The Bouncer, Treasurer, Keymaster and Inspector are not owners of it.
- **D3, rejecting deep imports: the `exports` map and a conformance import scan now (a and b); a declared list of cross-member contracts later with GAP-080 (c).**

#### Inspector decisions (Dale, chat, 2026-10-08):
- **D-A, `packages/telemetry`:** stays its own package, hosted by the Inspector (it is already in `hostedIn`); it is not folded in. Dale's condition: its files must be cleanly the Inspector's, which they are (the package holds only `initTelemetry`).
- **D-B, `packages/triage`:** the reserved-package mapping is dropped (Dale). Checked against the code on 2026-10-08, the package held only a README and a `package.json` saying "no build yet", so it was **removed** (Dale, chat, 2026-10-08: "go ahead and remove it"). Nothing imported it. The intent it recorded, an alert router for crashes, is GAP-107. The Registrar's built-in list still has a `system-doctor` entry with no code behind it (GAP-114). An earlier draft of this task gave the folder a new platform role, `builtin-agent`, on the mistaken belief that it was a shipped agent; that role was not kept.
- **D-C, `/api/v1/metrics`:** stays in `app.ts`; the cross-member aggregate is registered as GAP-106.
- **D-D, the alerting wording:** registered as GAP-107; nothing is built and no wording is changed now.

| Member | `control-plane/src` | `gatekeeper-egress/src` |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Landlord | `runs.ts`, `runtime.ts`, `runtime-docker.ts`, `runtime-ecs.ts`, `callbacks.ts`, `queues.ts`; `aws/*` and `gcp/*` (image build, IAM and task or job registration for a deployed agent; `aws/codebuild.ts` is also Registrar's, see `split`); the wake and run routes in `app.ts` | |
| Bouncer | the approvals and holds routes in `app.ts` (the egress calls its contract and enforces the answer; that is the Gatekeeper's code) | |
| Timekeeper | `schedules.ts` (the `/api/v1/schedules` handler; the rule, store and clock are in `packages/timekeeper`) | |
| Registrar | `catalog.ts`, `config-store.ts`, `skills.ts`, `skill-checks.ts`, `source.ts`, `systems.ts`; the registry and deploy routes in `app.ts` | |
| Keymaster | `connections.ts`, `credentials.ts` | `signin-links.ts` |
| Inspector | `progress-routes.ts`, `stream.ts` (the handlers; the hub, bus sinks and progress ring are in `packages/inspector`); the metrics and triage routes in `app.ts` | |
| Auditor | the ledger routes in `app.ts` | |
| Treasurer | the spend routes in `app.ts` (the egress calls its standing contract; that is the Gatekeeper's code) | |
| Gatekeeper | `policy.ts`, `identity-links.ts` (authentication data, D2 of the Bouncer flow) | `gatekeeper-egress.ts` (routes, hosts, credential injection), `main.ts` |
| Executive | | `meter.ts`, `models.ts`, `sigv4.ts` (read: used only by `models.ts`) |
| Composition root | `index.ts` | `index.ts` |
| Operator UI (a platform role, not a member) | `ui.ts` (the dashboard page served at `/ui`) | |

`app.ts` (about 2,100 lines) and `gatekeeper-egress.ts` (about 1,070) are shared by several members; breaking them up is where most of the extraction happens. A file several members share is listed in each owner's `owns` and in `SPLIT_FILES` (`app.ts`, `gatekeeper-egress.ts`, `aws/codebuild.ts`); breaking one up removes it from `SPLIT_FILES`. TSK-093 made `hostedIn` agree with the map: Keymaster, Gatekeeper and Auditor now list the shared packages whose files they own.

**Rules for every extraction.**
- Two lanes. Anything that edits `packages/control-plane/src/app.ts` runs in series with every other task that edits it. Work confined to another package may run in parallel.
- Moves change no behavior; new behavior is its own later task. Every task is green on its own.
- One plan per member, written when its turn comes, in the Treasurer shape: create the package and move the rule; move the data; define the contract; wire the callers; add a guard against a second copy; close out §6.15. Decisions that are Dale's are written as "Confirmed by Dale" before any task is handed to an executor; the plan is checked against the code by someone other than its author before handoff.
- Do not run an executor in a clone that another session is using; give each its own clone or worktree.

**Bouncer decisions already made by Dale (chat, 2026-10-03).** Authentication stays with the Gatekeeper and authorization belongs to the Bouncer. There must be an `agent-owner` role and every agent can identify its owner or owners. Access is role-based in all cases: users map to roles and roles map to privileges, with no direct user-to-privilege mapping. The actor and the approver are separate parties; for an agent's action the agent is the actor and the requesting user is the reviewer and approver, and for each other privileged action type the plan states who the parties are. Decided for the Bouncer plan (Dale, chat, 2026-10-03): the role-to-privilege table is built into the code now, and making it factory data that an admin edits is a later enhancement; an agent's owners are stored on its configuration record (SK3); putting the control plane behind the Gatekeeper is out of the Bouncer flow; the Gatekeeper's ingress passes the Discord author id with a wake and an admin links Discord ids to people; a run with no requesting user is decided by any owner of the agent; the `agent-owner` role holds the rights to decide held actions, view and export the agent's configuration, supply its credentials, and wake, pause and resume it; and the global `admin` and `approver` roles keep deciding any agent's held action. The plan and its remaining open items are in the Bouncer flow work stream (§5).
